Michaela watched the room's attention sharpen as he laid it out, watched Giles go very still in the way he went still when something confirmed a suspicion he hadn't wanted confirmed.

"And your read," Giles said, when Jonathan finished, "is not escape."

"My read," Jonathan said, and here his voice did something Michaela hadn't heard from him yet, something quieter and more careful, "is that it might just be watching exits because it learned to and doesn't know how to stop, the way people do, after. Not because it's planning anything. Because it can't turn it off." A pause. "I'd know something about that."

Nobody said anything for a moment. Giles looked at Jonathan with an expression that had gone, briefly, soft.

"That's — extremely useful, Jonathan," Giles said. "Thank you."

The door opened again. Benjy Sheridan came in with Misty Pantine a half-step behind her, and Michaela watched an eleven-year-old First Sergeant walk to the front of the room, set down a folded sheet of paper with actual casualty numbers on it — not the softened version, the real one — and deliver it standing at something that was, unmistakably, parade rest, before anyone had even asked her to report.

"Casualty and status report from the Irregulars, ma'am," Benjy said. "Full numbers. I didn't round anything down."

Michaela took the paper. Read it. Looked at the girl in front of her, eleven years old, steady, having clearly decided sometime in the last three days that steady was a thing you built rather than a thing you waited to feel.

"Thank you, First Sergeant," Michaela said, and meant every syllable of the rank.

The room kept filling. Aura with her notebook, relaying an update from Xander's room that she delivered in the flat precise register of someone who'd made logging into a discipline rather than a hobby. A runner from the school confirming the TX's status — unchanged, still, watching. Zabuto asking Giles a low quiet question about Kendra that Michaela didn't catch and didn't try to.

At two minutes to eight, the door opened one more time, and the room's temperature changed.

Michaela clocked it before she consciously understood why — the shift in air pressure that happened when something old walked into a space, the kind of old that made ordinary predators go quiet out of instinct rather than calculation. Two teenagers stood in the doorway. A boy, maybe fifteen, with the stillness of something that had learned to hold very still for centuries and kept the habit out of preference rather than necessity. A girl beside him, sixteen maybe, moving like she'd spent a very long time in spaces where the geometry itself had been hostile, and had simply stopped noticing.

Giles went the kind of still that meant something had just become more complicated.

"Baroness," he said.

"Watcher," the girl said, and something passed between them that had centuries stacked on one side of the conversation and considerable hard-won experience stacked on the other.

"You know them," Michaela said.

"Not personally," Giles said, still watching the girl with the careful voice he used for things that required precision. "By reputation. The Count, whose current era name is—"

"Blahdivastri," the boy said. "Savros Hieronymus. Your records will show Iverson. Freddy Iverson." A pause that did several things at once. "That era is concluded."

"And you," Giles said, to the girl.

"Veronique de Alençon," she said. "Currently Veronica Daley, although similarly — concluded." She looked around the room, taking in Buffy, Benjy, Jonathan, all of it, cataloguing with the efficiency of someone who'd already decided what she needed to say and was simply confirming her audience before saying it. "We are also, at present, the only entities in this city who know the complete floor plan of Squisher's basement, the identity of the individual managing the Mayor's supernatural intelligence network, the location of four of the seven illegal safe houses your surveillance has not yet found, and the prior cache location the Terminator used before it adopted the Kendall girl's body." Her gaze settled on Michaela. "We did not come here to be a threat. We came because we have information relevant to your current situation, and we have an interest in how it resolves."

The room held its breath in the way rooms do right before somebody senior decides which way this goes.

Michaela looked at Giles. Giles looked back with an expression that said, clearly, your call, my read is cautiously proceed, and this is Sunnydale, Captain, act accordingly.

She looked at Brockhurst.

He looked at the two teenagers in the doorway with the mild expression he reserved for things that were not remotely mild.

"You said you know who manages the Mayor's network," Brockhurst said.

"Trask," Veronique said. "Older than he appears. Considerably more capable than his manner suggests. And currently reporting to City Hall about the contents of this exact meeting, which he became aware of approximately forty minutes ago."

The room's quality changed again, sharper this time.

"Sit down," Michaela said. "Both of you." She glanced at Joyce, who was already reaching for the bread. "Mrs. Summers — does anyone want garlic bread."

"Of course," Joyce said, with the perfect, unshakeable calm of a woman who had made her peace with this town some time ago and intended to keep everyone fed through whatever came next regardless.

The Board, Michaela thought, watching the room settle — Buffy and Jonathan and Warren and Seven and Benjy and Misty and now two vampires older than most nations, all of it converging into one overcrowded conference room that smelled like solvent and garlic bread — was, against every reasonable expectation, actually assembling.

"All right," she said, and the room quieted for her. "Let's start with what everyone's found."

✳

Chapter 71: The Water Stain Has Views

"A man watching the ceiling is a man who has run out of better places to put his fear." — attributed to a Sunnydale Memorial night-shift nurse

Monday, November 3, 1997: South Marion Drive, Sunnydale Medical Complex, Room 412, Sunnydale, Evening 8:04pm —

The water stain on the ceiling had, over the past four days, become the single most reliable fixed point in Xander Harris's entire world, which said something uncomfortable about the current state of his world that he'd decided not to examine too closely.

It had a shape. Not a good shape — not a rabbit or a state he recognized or anything satisfying like that — just an irregular brown continent of old water damage that he'd memorized down to the smaller peninsula near the light fixture, because memorizing it gave his brain something to do that wasn't running the seventeen things or the seven contingency plans or the quiet dread that lived under both of those, the one he wasn't naming, the one that showed up whenever he let himself think too long about a girl in a paper gown eleven miles away doing math on a piece of spring steel.

"You're doing the hand thing," Aura said, not looking up from the notebook.

Xander looked down. His left hand was, in fact, doing the thing — trigger assembly check, thumb and forefinger where a selector switch would be if he were holding something that had a selector switch, which he was not, because he was holding a hospital blanket, which did not require safety checks of any kind.

He didn't know exactly when the hand had learned to do that on its own. Didn't let himself trace it back further than "sometime this week," because tracing it back further meant a specific house on a specific night, and a specific reason his hands had needed something to check and recheck that had nothing to do with any weapon at all, and that was a hallway he did not currently have the floor space to walk down, not with eleven miles and a countdown clock between him and the one person who'd have understood exactly why without him having to explain it.

He made it stop.

"Eleven times today," Aura said, writing the number down with the same flat precision she applied to everything since November first. "Down from nineteen yesterday. Ramesh would probably call that progress if I told him, which I'm not going to, because he'd want to know why I'm counting in the first place, and 'methodology' only covers so much conversational ground with a licensed physician."

"You could just tell him the truth."

"The truth is I started logging your hardware checks because it was something concrete I could do while everyone else was doing things I couldn't do, and it turned out to actually be useful, and now I can't stop, because stopping feels like admitting the logs were never really about the checks." She looked up, finally, and there was something unguarded in it that Aura Breckenridge — former social lieutenant, professional performer of not-noticing — allowed herself increasingly rarely. "They were about knowing something was still working. About you specifically. In a system where a lot of things weren't."

Xander looked at her for a moment.

"Thank you," he said. "For the logs. All of it."

"It's in the methodology," Aura said, which had become, over four days, the phrase that meant something else entirely, and they both let it sit there without either of them pushing on it further.

His phone — the one Michaela's people had left him, against Ramesh's specific and vocal objections, on the theory that a seventeen-year-old who'd already survived two gunshot wounds and a Terminator engagement this month probably deserved operational awareness more than he deserved bed rest — buzzed once on the side table.

Aura picked it up, read it, and her expression did something complicated.

"Pooka," she said. "Full debrief just came in through Moseby. Chase is working a second plan. Don't move before oh-six-hundred. There's something about a backup archive and an access code, and—" she paused, and Xander watched her decide, deliberately, to say the rest of it without softening it. "She said to tell you she'll be home by morning."

Xander looked at the water stain. At the peninsula near the light fixture that he now knew better than his own bedroom ceiling.

He believed her. That part came easy, came without having to work for it — Cordelia Chase did not say things she didn't intend to make true, had never once in the two years he'd properly known her, said a thing lightly that she meant heavily.

He was also, in the same breath, running the contingency for if she wasn't, because she would be the very first person to tell him that belief wasn't a plan, and being right about people was one of the seventeen things on the growing list of reasons he wasn't currently allowed to lose her.

"The meeting's happening right now," he said. Not a question.

"One floor down. Started four minutes ago." Aura checked her watch, the precise gesture of someone tracking multiple timelines simultaneously and keeping them all straight without apparent effort. "You're not invited, on account of Ramesh's extremely detailed instructions to the MPs about you staying in this bed."

"Ramesh didn't say anything about the phone."

"No," Aura agreed, a faint, careful smile finding the corner of her mouth. "He did not."

Xander picked up the phone. Looked at the screen. At Michaela's number, already saved, already trusted in a way that would have seemed insane to him two weeks ago and now just felt like Tuesday.

"The Kendalls," he said, before he dialed. "I want them in the Board. Actually in it. Not managed from a distance."

Aura's pen paused. "Why now, specifically."

"Because Cordelia's coming home tomorrow morning," Xander said, "and when she does, the first thing she's going to ask about is whether we did the actual work while she was gone, or just held the fort. And bringing the Kendalls in properly — not the operational picture, just knowing their kid's being looked after by people who give a damn — that's actual work. That's not holding anything. That's building something."

Aura looked at him for a long moment. Wrote something down.

"That's Monday meeting material," she said.

"Everything's Monday meeting material."

"That's why there's a Monday meeting," Aura said, and there was real warmth in it now, unguarded, the kind she'd have performed not having six months ago and simply had, plainly, tonight.

Xander dialed. Waited for Michaela to pick up, one floor below, in a room full of people who were, without his help, without Cordelia's help, building the exact thing the two of them had started without ever quite meaning to.

The water stain watched him do it, the way it had watched everything for four days, patient, brown, unbothered by any of it.

He was, he thought, going to have a lot to say to that ceiling someday, if he ever fully explained this month to anyone who hadn't lived it.

The line connected.

"Reeves."

"It's Xander," he said. "I've got something for the meeting. It's about the Kendalls."

✳

Chapter 72: A Phone Ringing in the Middle of Everything Is Never Good News, Except When It Is

"The most dangerous moment in any operation is the one where everyone in the room agrees on something. Check twice." — Murphy's Laws of Combat

Monday, November 3, 1997: South Marion Drive, Sunnydale Medical Complex, Conference Room B, Sunnydale, Evening 8:09pm —

Michaela's phone buzzed against the table in the rattling way that meant she'd left it on vibrate and forgotten to also silence it, which was a small operational failure she filed away to correct later, and which currently had the entire effect of stopping Veronique de Alençon mid-sentence about Squisher's basement floor plan.

"Reeves," Michaela said, answering it without leaving the table, because in a room this crowded stepping out for a phone call felt like conceding ground she wasn't sure she'd get back.

"It's Xander," the voice said, thin through the small speaker but unmistakably itself — steady, direct, the voice of somebody who'd learned very recently and very completely how to sound calm regardless of what his actual heart rate was doing. "I've got something for the meeting. It's about the Kendalls."

Michaela put the call on speaker without asking anyone's permission, because the room had earned the right to hear this together, and because she'd learned, over three days, that half of what made this Board work was that nobody in it hoarded information as leverage.

"Go ahead," she said. "You're on with everyone."

There was a small pause — Michaela pictured him one floor up, phone against his ear, MPs outside the door pretending very hard not to notice — and then he said it, plainly, the way he apparently said everything now: "I want them brought all the way in. Not managed at arm's length. Not the operational picture, just — they should know their daughter's being looked after by people who actually give a damn about her, not just about what she is now. That's not a security risk. That's the whole point of what we're building."

Giles, across the table, had gone quiet in the way he went quiet when something landed exactly where it needed to. Dawn's pen had stopped moving.

"They lost their daughter to something they don't have a framework for," Xander went on, "and instead of grieving her they've been managed, politely, at a distance, by adults who kept telling them it was for their own good. I think that's backwards. I think grief that gets handled at arm's length just curdles into something worse. I think you bring people in, and you tell them the truth they can handle, and you let them decide what to do with it, same as we did with Rona Williams."

"Rona Williams hasn't been introduced to this room yet," Brockhurst said, mildly, to no one in particular, and Michaela made a note to fix that oversight before the night was out.

"The Kendalls deserve the same offer," Xander said. "Come to a Monday meeting. See the board. Meet the people. Decide for themselves whether they want in, and if they do, they're in — not as liability management, as family."

Veronique, across the table, was watching the phone with an expression Michaela couldn't fully read — old, assessing, and something else underneath it that might, in a face eight hundred years less practiced at concealment, have been approval.

"That is," Veronique said, quietly, to Blahd, "precisely the kind of thing I was hoping to hear from this room."

Giles cleared his throat. "Xander. I concur completely. I should have proposed it myself days ago, and I— I didn't, because I let myself believe managing the Kendalls' access was protecting them, when in fact it was simply easier for me." A pause, and something that might have been genuine self-reproach moved across his face. "Thank you for saying it plainly."

"Somebody had to," Xander said. "Also, quick unrelated thing — Pooka's debrief just came through. Cordelia's working a second plan. Nobody moves before oh-six-hundred. She says she'll be home by morning."

The room went very still.

Michaela looked at the faces around the table — Buffy, Benjy, Jonathan, Warren and Seven, two vampires who'd walked in ten minutes ago carrying centuries of leverage and had somehow ended up, in the space of that single sentence, looking like everyone else in the room: quietly, fiercely relieved.

"Copy that," Michaela said, into the phone, keeping her own voice level with the ease of long practice. "Oh-six-hundred. Tell her — tell her the Board's holding."

"I'll tell her," Xander said, and there was something in the way he said it, something that had nothing to do with tactics or timelines, that made even Zabuto, at the far end of the table, look briefly, unexpectedly moved.

"Get some rest, Harris," Michaela added.

"Working on it," Xander said, in the exact tone of somebody who had absolutely no intention of resting, and hung up.

Michaela set the phone down. Looked around the table — at Giles, already making a note to contact the Kendalls in the morning; at Dawn, adding a new line to whatever page the notebook was currently on; at Blahd and Veronique, settling into the room in a way that suggested they'd just decided, definitively, that this was a table worth sitting at.

"All right," she said. "The Kendalls join the Board, effective this week. I want that handled with care, not procedure." She looked at the clock on the wall, at the eleven hours standing between now and oh-six-hundred, at the weight of every single thread in this room converging toward the same quiet, unbearable countdown. "Now. Where were we."

"Squisher's basement," Veronique said, and the meeting went on.

✳

Chapter 73: The Mayor Keeps Better Notes Than People Expect

"A tidy desk is the sign of a mind that has already decided what matters." — attributed to Albert Einstein, disputed

Monday, November 3, 1997: Sunnydale City Hall, Mayor's Office, Evening 8:15pm —

Richard Wilkins III had a very tidy desk, which was, in Trask's professional experience, the single most reliable indicator that a man was dangerous. Messy men had priorities they hadn't sorted yet. Tidy men had already decided what mattered and filed everything else, and the Mayor's desk had exactly three items on it tonight: a legal pad, a fountain pen, and a small dish of butterscotch candies that he offered to absolutely everyone who walked into the office, including, on one memorable occasion two years ago, a vampire he was in the process of having quietly disposed of.

"Trask," the Mayor said, warm, unhurried, the way he said everything, "sit down, take a candy, and tell me about my evening."

Trask sat. Did not take a candy, on the theory that a hundred and forty years of professional caution had taught him that accepting things from Richard Wilkins, however small, tended to come with strings he wouldn't notice until they were already load-bearing.

"There's a meeting happening right now at the hospital," Trask said. "Conference Room B. Reeves's people, the Watcher, most of what's left of the original Halloween cohort, and—" he paused, because this part mattered and he wanted the Mayor to hear it land properly, "—Blahdivastri and de Alençon walked in ten minutes ago. Voluntarily. Offering full cooperation."

The Mayor's pen stopped moving.

That was the tell, with Wilkins — not a raised voice, not a flicker of temper, just the pen going still for exactly as long as it took him to fully recalculate. Trask had learned to watch for it the way other men watched a poker opponent's hands.

"Now that," the Mayor said, slowly, setting the pen down entirely, "is interesting. Old Savros hasn't stuck his nose above ground in — what, has it been sixty years since the Hendricks unpleasantness? And now he's walking into a hospital conference room offering the Board his floor plans." He picked up a butterscotch candy for himself, unwrapped it with the unhurried patience of a man who enjoyed small ceremonies. "What do you make of that, Trask?"

"I make of it that they've decided the Board's worth more as an ally than a curiosity," Trask said. "And I make of it that if a nine-hundred-year-old vampire has decided that, sir, it's worth us taking seriously too."

"Mm." The Mayor let the candy sit in his cheek, thoughtful. "You know what I like about this town, Trask? Really like, deep down, in the part of me that's been planning this Ascension a hundred years and counting?"

"No, sir."

"Everybody in it eventually shows their whole hand, if you're patient enough to watch." He looked out the window, at Sunnydale doing its evening thing, lit up and ordinary and utterly unaware, same as always, of how much of it Richard Wilkins had already quietly purchased, permitted, or personally arranged over the decades. "The Board's growing a spine faster than I expected. Aristocrat vampires. A TX-a-hundred-and-something that's apparently gone soft in a school library. A pack of feral children living in my abandoned amusement park who I am reliably informed have been teaching a fox to fetch." He said this last part with the delighted disbelief of a man who found the whole spectacle, on some level, entertaining. "And Maggie Walsh out at that research facility, thinking she's the only one in town running a secret program."

"Should we be concerned about Walsh specifically, sir?"

"Everybody should always be a little concerned about Maggie Walsh," the Mayor said, mildly. "But no. Not tonight. Walsh's timeline's about to collapse on her whether we lift a finger or not — I hear the writs go out at oh-eight-hundred, and I hear her federal judge friend's about to discover his usual rulings don't cover quite what he thinks they cover." He smiled, the pleasant, unbothered smile that Trask had watched precede exactly nothing good for a hundred and forty years running. "No, what I'm interested in is the Board. I want you watching them, Trask. Closely. Not interfering — not yet, we're a long way off from needing to interfere — just watching. I want to know everything they do, everyone they bring in, every alliance they strike, right up until the day it actually matters."

"And when does it matter, sir?"

The Mayor looked at him, and for just a moment the pleasant unhurried mask slipped enough to show something older and colder underneath, something that had been patient for a hundred years because patience, applied correctly, always won.

"June," he said. "It matters in June. Everything between now and then is just watching people build the thing I'm eventually going to have to take apart." He popped the rest of the candy, unwrapped another, offered the dish again out of pure habit though Trask still declined. "In the meantime, Trask — send my regards to Mr. Giles. And find out how the vampires are financing that manor renovation. A man likes to know where the money's coming from in his own town."

"Yes, sir," Trask said, and rose to go, carrying with him the unease he always carried out of this office — not fear exactly, Trask had stopped fearing much of anything a long time ago, but the sober professional respect of a man who'd worked for exactly one employer patient enough to plan a hundred years ahead, and intended to keep working for him precisely because that patience terrified everyone who properly understood it.

Behind him, Richard Wilkins picked up his pen, made one small unhurried note on the legal pad, and went back to looking, for all the world, like a man with absolutely nothing but time.

✳

Chapter 74: The Room Faith Picked Because It Had Two Exits

"Choose your room the way you'd choose your ground: by the exits, not the view." — Sun Tzu, paraphrased

Monday, November 3, 1997: The Sunnydale Arms, Room 8, Evening 8:20pm —

Faith Lehane had picked room eight for two reasons. First, it was on the ground floor, which meant a window that opened onto an alley instead of a forty-foot drop, and Faith had developed, over the past several months of not having anywhere permanent to sleep, a very firm policy about always knowing the fast way out of any room she was in. Second, it was eleven dollars a night, which was eleven dollars more than she'd had a reliable source of income for since her Watcher stopped being alive.

She didn't think about that second part more than she had to. Some doors you kept shut on purpose.

The room had a bed, a chair, a TV that got four channels if you stood in exactly the right spot holding the antenna, and a mirror on the closet door that Faith had turned to face the wall on her first night here, for reasons she also didn't examine too closely, except that catching her own reflection unexpectedly at two in the morning had a way of making her feel like she was looking at someone she used to know better.

She was doing pushups when the knock came. Not because she'd heard anything approaching — she'd have heard that regardless, Slayer hearing didn't take breaks for exercise — but because pushups were a thing to do with her body while her head did the other thing, the thing where it ran back over the last four months on a loop she couldn't seem to turn off no matter how tired she got.

The knock was soft. Deliberate. Not cop-knock, not landlord-knock. Someone who'd decided, specifically, to knock gently.

Faith was at the door with a stake in her hand before she'd consciously decided to move, because that was just how her body worked now, had worked since Kakistos, would probably keep working that way for the rest of whatever her life turned out to be.

She checked the peephole. A girl, blonde, maybe her own age, standing back from the door with her hands visible and empty in a way that Faith clocked immediately as deliberate — somebody who knew exactly what kind of nerves she might be walking up on and had decided to make herself as unthreatening as possible before knocking.

Faith opened the door on the chain first.

"Yeah?"

"Faith?" The blonde girl's voice was careful, not scared exactly, just measured. "I'm Buffy. Buffy Summers."

Faith knew the name. Everybody who'd been Called in the last two years knew the name, the way you knew the names of people who'd survived things you hadn't survived yet — with a specific mix of respect and resentment that she wasn't proud of and hadn't found a way to fully talk herself out of.

"The Sunnydale Slayer," Faith said. "Word gets around."

"Zabuto told my Watcher you'd come through town a few days ago." Buffy's hands stayed visible, still, at her sides. "I wanted to find you before somebody else did. Before you got the version of this town where everyone treats you like competition."

Faith almost laughed. It came out flatter than she meant it to. "That's kind of exactly what I figured this was."

"It's not what I want it to be," Buffy said. "Can I come in? Or — we could talk out here, whatever's easier. I'm not trying to corner you."

Faith looked at her for a long moment through the gap the chain allowed. Assessed her the way she'd learned to assess everyone lately — quick, thorough, looking for the tell that said this was a setup, a test, one more person who was going to smile at her right up until the moment they didn't need to anymore.

She didn't find one. That unsettled her more than finding one would have.

Faith unhooked the chain.

Buffy came in, glanced once at the mirror turned to the wall — didn't comment on it, which Faith noted and filed as a point in her favor — and sat, uninvited but careful about it, on the edge of the single chair, leaving Faith the bed.

"I'm not here to tell you Sunnydale's got room for exactly one Slayer," Buffy said, before Faith could get out whatever defensive thing was already building in her chest. "I'm here because there's a whole thing happening in this town right now — a group of people building something real, against something dangerous — and it's built on the idea that everybody brings what they've got and nobody's asked to be less than they are to fit." She paused. "I've got Kendra here too. Three of us. That's never happened before, and I don't fully know what it means yet, but I know it doesn't have to mean a fight over territory. It could just mean three people who understand something nobody else in this town does, finally not doing it completely alone."

Faith looked at the floor. At her own hands, resting on her knees, steadier than she felt.

"You don't know me," she said, quiet. "You don't know what I—" she stopped. Started again, different angle, because the first sentence had been heading somewhere she wasn't ready to walk yet, not with a stranger, not tonight. "My Watcher's dead. Diana Dormer. Six weeks ago. I've been doing this alone since, and alone's — alone's fine. I'm good at alone."

"I believe you're good at it," Buffy said, gently. "I don't think that's the same as it being good for you."

Faith's jaw tightened. She almost said something sharp, the reflexive thing, the thing that would put distance back between them the way distance had kept her breathing this whole time.

She didn't say it.

"There's a meeting tonight," Buffy said, into the silence, not pushing, just offering it and letting it sit there. "Not tonight-tonight — it already started, actually, I'm supposed to be there right now, honestly — but there's one every Monday. A board on a wall. People who show up and do the work and don't ask you to explain yourself first." A small, careful smile. "Joyce — my mom — makes way too much food. You could come by sometime. No pressure on when."

Faith looked at her. At the unforced sincerity in it, the total absence of the performance Faith had been braced for since the knock.

"Why're you doing this," Faith said. "Really. What's it to you if some other Slayer's crashing in a motel across town."

Buffy was quiet for a moment, like she was actually weighing the honest answer instead of reaching for the easy one.

"Because two years ago I would've given anything for somebody to knock on my door instead of just letting me figure it out completely by myself," she said. "And because I think there's more than enough dark in this town for three of us, and none of it gets easier if we're all carrying it separately."

Faith didn't say anything for a long moment.

"I'll think about it," she said, finally, which was as much ground as she had in her to give tonight, and Buffy, to her credit, didn't push for more.

"That's all I'm asking," Buffy said, standing. She got to the door, paused, hand on the frame. "For what it's worth — Diana Dormer. I'm sorry. That's not a small thing to be carrying."

Faith looked at the turned-around mirror on the closet door and said nothing, because there wasn't a version of an answer that wouldn't have cracked something she wasn't ready to have crack yet.

Buffy let herself out, quiet, and Faith sat alone in room eight with two exits and a stake still loose in one hand, and thought, for the first time in six weeks, that maybe alone wasn't actually a policy she'd chosen so much as a wall she'd built because nobody had bothered knocking yet.

She didn't cry. She wasn't there yet.

But she didn't turn the TV on either, and she sat with that instead, for a long time, in the quiet.

✳

Chapter 75: A Century and Change of Deciding Otherwise

"A century is a long apprenticeship in patience, and patience is the only weapon that never runs out of ammunition." — attributed to a Watcher's Council training manual

Monday, November 3, 1997: Chestnut Street Office, Sunnydale, Evening 8:30pm —

The office on Chestnut Street had been, until three days ago, an empty storefront that had cycled through a nail salon, a failed video rental place, and eight months of nothing at all, and it now held four desks, a whiteboard nobody had gotten around to labeling properly yet, and Sheila Martini, who was standing at the window with her arms crossed, watching the street outside with the stillness of someone who had spent a very long time learning to be unnoticed and had never quite unlearned it.

"You don't have to stand guard," Giles said, from the desk where he was going through a box of files Zabuto had brought over. "Joel warded the whole building this afternoon. Threefold, he said, whatever that means precisely."

"Old habits." Sheila didn't turn from the window. Her voice had the faint, careful accent of somewhere that had stopped existing as a place two centuries ago, worn smooth at the edges by a hundred years of living in places that weren't home. "Hundred and thirty years of watching doors doesn't just switch off 'cause some kid drew a rune on a threshold. Trust me, I've tried turning it off. Doesn't work. Believe me, I'd love to just sit down and relax like a normal person for five fucking minutes."

Giles set the file down. Looked at her properly for the first time all evening, past the ordinary shorthand they'd fallen into over these three days.

"May I ask you something," he said, "that isn't operational."

Sheila's shoulders did something small and guarded. "You may ask. No promises I'll answer without being an ass about it first."

"What was it like," Giles said, carefully, "being in Spike's court, before. Not the details, if you'd rather not — just. What made you the one who chose differently, when the others didn't."

Sheila was quiet for a long moment, still watching the street, though Giles suspected she wasn't seeing it anymore.

"Turned in 1867," she said, finally. "By something a hell of a lot less charming than Spike, in a century that gave a woman exactly two options if she wanted anything resembling power, and both of 'em involved a man deciding to hand some to her." A pause. "Spent the first forty years exactly the way you'd expect. Not gonna dress that up for either of our comfort."

"I wasn't asking you to."

"I know." She glanced at him, briefly, something almost like gratitude in it. "Spike's court took me in around 1930. Liked collecting things with a bit of history, a bit of style. I fit the bill." Her mouth did something that wasn't quite a smile. "Got real good at being whatever a room needed. It's a survival skill. Stops bein' a choice somewhere around year sixty and just becomes the whole act, top to bottom."

"And Halloween."

"Halloween was the first night in a hundred and thirty years somethin' handed me the chance to be somebody else entirely, and turns out — surprise, apparently — the actual me underneath all that performing had opinions the whole damn time. Just never once got permission to open her mouth about 'em." She finally turned from the window, something raw in her face Giles hadn't seen there before. "Chose different because for one night, different was actually on the table, and I found out real fast I couldn't go back to pretending it wasn't, after."

Giles was quiet for a moment, absorbing that.

"That took considerable courage," he said. "Whatever the spell's mechanics were. The choice underneath it was still yours."

"Was it, though." Real uncertainty in it now, the kind that had clearly been sitting under the surface for three days with nowhere safe to land. "Or did the spell just hand me permission I'd never have granted myself in a hundred and thirty years, and I've just been runnin' on borrowed permission ever since, waitin' for somebody to notice I'm still exactly the same sorry piece of work underneath it."

"I don't believe that," Giles said, simply.

"You don't know me well enough yet to believe or disbelieve shit about me, Mr. Giles."

"Perhaps not." Giles looked at her steadily. "But I've spent a great many years around Watchers who believed a person's nature got fixed the moment they were turned, and I've found that belief wrong nearly every single time. What you do with a hundred and thirty years counts for a great deal more than what happened in the first one. You've spent three days doing logistics, warding schedules, keeping this office running while everyone else gets credit for the flashier parts of what we're building. That's not the behavior of somebody waiting around to revert."

Sheila looked at him for a long moment, something working behind her eyes she clearly hadn't expected to be examining tonight, over a box of files.

"You're awful direct, for a librarian," she said, finally, warmth creeping back into it, careful but genuine.

"I've been told that's a recent development," Giles said, something in his own expression suggesting he knew exactly which recent events had caused it.

The bell over the door chimed. Joel Garrity came in, staff in hand, looking pleasantly surprised none of an entire afternoon's worth of Latin had gone wrong.

"Wards are holding," he reported, to no one in particular, then, catching the quality of the room's silence, glanced between Giles and Sheila. "Am I interrupting something."

"Nah," Sheila said, and this time the almost-smile made it all the way to something real. "Not interrupting a damn thing. Good work on the wards, kid."

Joel looked, briefly, unconvinced, decided not to pursue it, and set his staff by the door with the care of a man who treated his tools like they mattered, because in his case, increasingly, they did.

Giles went back to the file box. Sheila returned to the window, though something in her posture had shifted, slightly, the guard down a fraction it hadn't been down before.

Outside, Sunnydale went on being ordinary, oblivious, and the office on Chestnut Street held its quiet work a little longer into the November evening.

✳

Chapter 76: What a Watcher Trains For and What a Watcher Doesn't

"We train for the fight we expect and hope we never need what we didn't." — attributed to Lt. Col. Michael Sheridan, U.S. Army, 82nd Airborne, Ret.

Monday, November 3, 1997: Guest Room, Zabuto Residence, Sunnydale, Evening 8:35pm —

Kendra Young had made the bed that morning with hospital corners, the way she'd been trained to since she was nine years old, and she looked at it now — flat, precise, unslept-in in any way that would disturb the corners — and thought, not for the first time this week, that a made bed was possibly the least useful skill anyone had ever trained into her.

Mr. Zabuto knocked once, formally, before entering, which he always did, which was itself a small kindness she hadn't fully known how to receive the first few times it happened, because Sam Zabuto knocking on a door before entering the room of his own Slayer was not a thing that had ever occurred to her as necessary, back home, back before.

"You missed the evening katas," he said, not unkindly.

"I know, Mr. Zabuto." She said it plainly, without the flinch she'd have had two months ago at any suggestion she'd failed a discipline. "I was reading the case files on the Board instead."

He raised an eyebrow, the quiet permission-to-continue he'd developed since they'd arrived in Sunnydale, since everything had gotten stranger than anything the Council's training had prepared either of them for.

"Three Slayers in one town," Kendra said. "There is no precedent for this in the Council archives. I checked."

"There isn't," Zabuto agreed. "The Council has never had cause to develop a protocol for it, because the Council's entire system assumes exactly one Slayer active at any time, with the next Called only upon the current one's death." He sat, carefully, in the chair by the window, the way he always sat, the posture of a man who'd spent a career being very deliberate about every physical choice he made in front of his charge. "You've been trained your entire life to be singular. Irreplaceable in the way a weapon is irreplaceable — there is only one of you, and everything depends on that one."

"And now there are three."

"And now there are three," he confirmed. "And I confess, Kendra, I do not have a framework for what that means for you any more than the Council archives do."

Kendra sat on the edge of the perfectly made bed, disturbing the corners for the first time all day, and found she didn't mind as much as she'd have expected to.

"Buffy Summers has friends," she said, working through it slowly, the way she worked through anything unfamiliar — methodically, out loud, testing each piece before trusting it. "A crew. People who fight beside her and are not afraid to tell her when she is wrong. I have watched them, three days now. It is not what the Council trained me to expect a Slayer's life to look like."

"No," Zabuto said. "It isn't."

"Is that permitted?"

The question came out smaller than she meant it to, and she heard the smallness in it and hated it a little, the way she'd been trained to hate any sound that suggested uncertainty, weakness, a Slayer who needed to ask permission for something as basic as whether she was allowed to have people.

Zabuto was quiet for a long moment, and when he answered, his voice had a careful weight to it that she'd rarely heard from him before.

"Kendra," he said. "I was assigned to train a weapon. That is the truth of how the Council prepared me, and I will not pretend otherwise to you now, because you deserve honesty more than you deserve comfort." He met her eyes directly, which he did rarely, out of the same formal deliberateness that governed everything else about him. "But I have watched you, for sixteen years, and somewhere in the middle of training a weapon, I found I had also raised a person, whether the Council intended that outcome or not. And I find, watching this town, watching this thing Buffy Summers has built — I find I am no longer certain the Council's framework was ever correct to begin with."

Kendra looked at him. At the man who had been, for as long as she could remember, the entire architecture of what was permitted and what was not, correctness and its absence, duty stated so plainly it had never once occurred to her to question its foundations.

"You are saying I am permitted," she said, carefully, "to want what she has."

"I am saying," Zabuto said, "that I do not believe wanting connection makes you less of a Slayer. I have watched three days of evidence suggesting the opposite may in fact be true — that Buffy Summers fights better, not worse, for having people who would notice if she fell." He paused. "I do not know how to retrain sixteen years of doctrine in a week, Kendra. I am not certain I fully know how to retrain it in myself, either. But I will not stand in the way of you finding out whether there's a different way to carry this."

Kendra sat with that for a long time, the made bed disturbed now, the room quiet around them, the unfamiliar sensation of a door she hadn't known was closed swinging open by degrees she wasn't yet sure how to walk through.

"There is a meeting," she said, finally. "The Board's Monday meeting. Buffy invited me to attend, three days ago. I told her I would consider it."

"And have you considered it."

Kendra looked at her hands — Slayer's hands, trained since nine years old, capable of things she'd never fully allowed herself to feel proud of because pride wasn't part of the doctrine either.

"Yes," she said. "I would like to go. If that is permitted."

Zabuto's expression did something Kendra had never seen it do before — something that might, in a less formal man, have simply been called being moved.

"It's permitted," he said, quietly. "It's more than permitted, Kendra. I think it might be exactly right."

Outside, Sunnydale carried on with its ordinary evening, unaware of the small, quiet, enormous thing that had just shifted in a guest room on the far side of town — a Slayer trained sixteen years to be a weapon, discovering, carefully, that she was permitted to also simply be a girl who wanted to belong somewhere.

✳

Chapter 77: On the Board

"A board is just a table until enough people decide to sit at it honestly." — attributed to Rupert Giles, private correspondence

Monday, November 3, 1997: South Marion Drive, Sunnydale Medical Complex, outside Conference Room B, Sunnydale, Evening 9:40pm —

The meeting broke the way meetings broke when everyone in the room had somewhere else they urgently needed to be — not all at once, but in a slow uneven spill into the hallway, people peeling off in twos and threes, still finishing sentences they'd started at the table.

Zabuto was one of the last ones through the door, and he stopped Michaela with a small, formal incline of the head that she'd learned, over three days, meant he had a question he'd been holding back out of politeness and had finally decided the politeness cost more than it saved.

"Captain Reeves," he said. "Forgive me — I've been in this town three days and I confess I still don't have a name for what I'm looking at." He gestured, mildly, at the general spill of people into the corridor — Blahd and Veronique deep in quiet conversation with Giles, Benjy and Misty comparing notes with Dawn's notebook open between them, Jonathan and Tamara already halfway down the hall still arguing gap columns. "This. All of it. What is it called?"

Michaela opened her mouth to answer and found, for the first time in longer than she cared to admit, that she didn't have a ready answer. Black Company had a name. AFSOC had a name. This did not have a name, because nobody had ever needed one before tonight, when a hundred-and-something-year-old Watcher-in-training had asked the question plainly enough that the gap became impossible to ignore.

"It's not an operation," she said, slowly, working it out loud. "It's not a unit."

"It's not anything the Council has a category for either," Zabuto said. "Which is, I confess, part of why I'm asking."

"We're not an organization," Dawn said, from a few feet away, not looking up from the notebook, in the flat matter-of-fact tone of an eleven-year-old contributing to an adult conversation without asking permission first. "We're a corkboard with opinions."

That got a laugh out of Brockhurst — a short, genuine one, rare enough from him that several heads turned.

"She's not wrong," Joyce said, coming out of the room last with the empty garlic bread tray under one arm. "There is, very literally, a board. On my dining room wall. With a parking lot and an action column and Dawn's handwriting all over it."

"So what does that make us," Veronique said, with the dry precision of someone who found the whole exercise mildly amusing and was prepared to be entertained by wherever it landed. "The Parking Lot?"

"The Action Column," Blahd offered, deadpan, and Michaela caught the corner of his mouth doing something that, on a face nine hundred years old, passed for genuine delight.

"We could just be the Board," Xander said.

Everyone looked at the phone propped against Aura's ear, because that was where the voice had come from — Xander, still one floor up, still on the call he'd apparently never actually ended, listening in on speaker the whole time.

"You're still on the line," Michaela said.

"I've been on the line the whole meeting," Xander said, entirely unbothered. "Ramesh didn't say anything about staying on a call. Anyway. The Board. There's a board. We're the people who show up to it. Simple. Accurate. Doesn't require anybody to explain anything to anybody new, because you just point at the wall."

There was a pause in the hallway — the pause that happens right before a room decides, collectively and without quite meaning to, that something has landed.

"The Board," Giles repeated, testing the shape of it. "It's rather understated, for what we're actually doing."

"That's the point," Dawn said, still not looking up, already writing something in the notebook. "Nobody asks follow-up questions about a board. Everybody asks follow-up questions about a 'formation.'"

"I like it," Benjy said, with the decisiveness of a First Sergeant rendering a verdict. "It's not trying to sound like more than it is. That's — that's actually good tactics."

"High praise, coming from her," Misty said.

"I've never met a board I didn't respect," Veronique said, and something about the sheer incongruity of a woman who'd been alive since before printing presses existed weighing in seriously on corkboard nomenclature broke the last of the tension in the hallway into something warmer.

"The Board," Zabuto said again, quieter this time, like he was filing it away properly. "Very well. Thank you. That's precisely what I needed."

"Anytime, Mr. Zabuto," Xander's voice said, tinny through the phone speaker, and then, after a beat: "Somebody tell Cordelia when she's home. She's going to have opinions about whether she got a vote."

"She'll have opinions regardless of whether she got a vote," Aura said, already writing the exchange down in her own notebook, because apparently this, too, was going in the logs. "That's never stopped her before."

The hallway laughed, quietly, all of it — vampires and Watchers and pixies and eleven-year-old First Sergeants and a phone call from a hospital bed — and somewhere in that laugh, without anyone formally declaring it, the thing that had needed a name for three days finally, quietly, had one.

The Board.

It stuck immediately, the way the good ones always did — not because anyone had planned it, but because it was true, and because it was funny, and because, as Dawn pointed out later while updating the actual literal board with a new header that just read THE BOARD in her small precise handwriting, nobody ever asks follow-up questions about a corkboard.

✳

Chapter 78: Paladin and Cahill Come to Call

"Some debts don't get paid back. They get paid forward, to whoever's standing in front of you when you're finally able to." — attributed to Tor Hauer

Tuesday, November 4, 1997: Chestnut Street Office, Sunnydale; Afternoon 4:00pm —

Xander had his clipboard out and his feet up on the corner of his desk when the bell over the door went, and he didn't look up right away, because the bell had been going all afternoon — Board business, mostly, people finding their way to the new office out of curiosity more than necessity.

"Harris."

He looked up at that, because the voice belonged to someone he hadn't heard say his actual last name, flatly, without a locker involved, in longer than he generally let himself think about.

Tor Hauer stood in the doorway in the same black gunfighter's getup he'd apparently been wearing, on and off, since Halloween — hat, low-slung holster, the silver knight emblem — with Heidi a half-step behind him in her own matching black, the Winchester slung easy over one shoulder like it had simply become part of how she carried herself now.

"Tor." Xander didn't take his feet off the desk, which cost him something to manage, old instinct wanting badly to make himself smaller the way it always had around this doorway. "Heidi. This is a surprise."

"Figured it might be." Tor came in, unhurried, and set something on the corner of Xander's desk — an actual silver dollar, old, worn smooth. "That's for the locker, freshman year. The one with your bike lock still on it when I shoved you in. Never did pay you back for the lock."

"You don't have to—"

"I know I don't have to." Tor's voice was different than Xander remembered it — not gentler exactly, but more deliberate, the formal courtesy of a man who'd apparently absorbed rather more of Paladin's actual code than anyone had expected a costume to leave behind. "Paladin's got a saying about debts. Some of 'em don't get paid back to the person you owe 'em to. They get paid forward, to whoever needs it when you're finally able to give it." He nodded at the silver dollar. "That one's small. There's bigger ones I mean to get to."

Heidi settled into the doorway beside him, arms crossed, watching the exchange with the quiet attention of someone who'd clearly had this conversation rehearsed between them beforehand. "We heard you've got a board now," she said. "Heard it's got a name and everything."

"Word travels fast."

"Small town, big Hellmouth," Heidi said. "Word travels the way it always has. Faster now, if anything, since half the kids who went to Ethan's that night still can't put a gun down without their hands remembering how good it feels."

Xander finally took his feet off the desk. "You looking to sign up for something."

"Lookin' to offer," Tor said, correcting him, mild but precise about the distinction. "Difference matters to me. I'm not askin' you for a spot at your table, Harris. I'm telling you Heidi and I have got skills your Board's probably gonna need eventually, and we'd rather you know we're available before you need us bad enough to have to go lookin'."

"We're not the same assholes we were sophomore year," Heidi added, blunter about it than Tor had been, though something in her tone suggested she meant it just as seriously. "Figured that was worth saying out loud, since we never really got around to it properly. Sorry about the lockers. Sorry about a lot of stuff, actually, now that I've got some distance on it."

Xander considered them both for a long moment — the black hat, the Winchester, the formal weight two former bullies had apparently decided to carry now that a Halloween costume had handed them, of all things, an actual code of honor to live up to.

"Apology accepted," he said, finally, and meant it. "And yeah. We're gonna need you eventually. I don't know exactly what for yet, but I've got a corkboard that's gonna have your names on it by tonight, and I think that's actually going to turn out to matter, considering everything else on it."

"Good," Tor said, satisfied, and touched the brim of his hat, an old gesture that had apparently become natural to him. "We'll be around."

"Tell Cordelia we said hey," Heidi added, already turning to go. "Heard she's had one hell of a month. Figured she could use knowing more people are in her corner than she probably thinks."

They left the way they'd come, unhurried, and Xander sat looking at the silver dollar on his desk for a long moment before picking it up and setting it, carefully, in the drawer where he kept the things that actually mattered.

✳

Chapter 79: The Footage Problem

"The camera doesn't lie, but it also doesn't know what it's looking at." — attributed to a Sunnydale MHMR security memo

Monday, November 3, 1997: The Sunnydale Motor Inn, Room 14,
Sunnydale, Evening 8:40pm —

The Sunnydale Motor Inn did not, as a general policy, rent rooms by the week, and Carl Kolchak had needed to have a moderately creative conversation with the night manager three days ago to arrange otherwise, a conversation that had ultimately involved forty dollars cash and the manager's stated belief that whatever Kolchak was investigating was probably better left uninvestigated.

Kolchak had heard that sentiment a great many times, in a great many towns, over a great many years, and had never once found it to be sound professional advice.

"Play it again," Perry White said, from the other bed, where she'd set up the little portable monitor Jain had rigged to the camera three days ago and hadn't turned off since except to sleep, and not even then.

Jain, folded into the room's one chair with the patient posture of a man who'd filmed stranger things in his career than most people would believe and had learned to simply keep the tape rolling and ask questions afterward, rewound it without comment.

The gorilla filled the little screen again — twelve, maybe fifteen feet of very real, very solid primate, carrying a screaming blonde teenager in a blue-and-gold ball gown down Alpert Street at a dead run, knuckles striking pavement in a rhythm that had, Kolchak had noted at the time and noted again now, an almost military cadence to it.

"That is not a man in a suit," Perry said, for approximately the ninth time since Halloween. "I have seen men in suits, Carl. I covered the Puppet Master thing in '89, I know what a man in a suit looks like on tape. That thing's shoulders move like a real primate's shoulders move."

"I know," Kolchak said.

"So what do we do with it."

That was the actual question, the one they'd been circling for three days without landing on an answer either of them fully trusted, because the honest truth was that Kolchak had spent eleven years chasing exactly this kind of footage — the real thing, undeniable, four separate angles of impossible events happening to real, named, identifiable people in a real American town — and now that he finally had it sitting on a cassette in a motel room, he found himself, for the first time in his professional life, genuinely uncertain whether running it was the responsible thing to do.

"Greer wants it filed," Perry said. "I called him this morning. He wants it filed by Thursday, six-part series, 'The Sunnydale Files,' his words, and he's already talking to the syndication desk."

"And what do you want."

Perry was quiet for a moment, looking at the frozen frame of the gorilla mid-stride.

"I want to know what happens to that town if we run it," she said, finally. "I've been a reporter twenty-two years, Carl. I know what happens when a story this size drops on a town that isn't ready for it. Every kook and cult and government spook in the country descends inside a week. And there's kids in that footage. Real kids, with real names, who are gonna have their whole lives upended by strangers showing up at their high school wanting to know if they're secretly superheroes."

"There's also," Kolchak said, carefully, "the small matter of whatever's actually happening in that town continuing to happen, whether we report it or not. You saw the fires. You saw what came out of the ground behind the Bronze." He paused. "I've chased eleven years of stories that got buried, Perry. Every single time, official channels swore up and down that reporting it would only cause harm, and every single time, what actually happened was the thing kept happening in the dark, to people who never got warned."

"So you want to run it."

"I want," Kolchak said, slowly, "to talk to the people actually living inside it first. Before we decide anything." He looked at the screen — at the paused image of a boy in a torn Army costume who'd, according to what Kolchak had pieced together from three days of careful, patient, unofficial legwork, been shot twice and was currently recovering in Sunnydale Medical under conditions that read, to a man with eleven years of experience reading conditions like that, distinctly like a federal cover story in progress. "There's clearly some kind of formal apparatus already managing this. Somebody with real resources. If there's a responsible way to tell this story, it probably runs through them, not around them."

Perry looked at him for a long moment.

"You've gone soft, Kolchak," she said, but there wasn't any real heat in it.

"I've gone careful," Kolchak said. "There's a difference, and I earned it the hard way, same as you."

There was a knock at the door — three knocks, evenly spaced, deliberate in a way that made both of them go still, because ordinary knocks weren't usually that measured.

Kolchak checked the peephole. A woman he didn't recognize, dark-haired, standing back from the door with the stance of someone who'd knocked on a great many doors in a great many uncertain situations and had learned exactly how to stand so the person inside felt less threatened rather than more.

He opened it on the chain.

"Mr. Kolchak," the woman said. "Ms. White. My name is Michaela Reeves. I believe you've got some footage from Friday night that we should probably discuss — and I promise you, before you ask, I'm not here to confiscate anything or threaten your press credentials. I'm here because I think you're both about to make a decision that deserves better information than you currently have."

Kolchak looked at Perry.

Perry looked at the frozen gorilla on the little screen, and then at the door, and then, slowly, unhooked the chain herself.

"You'd better come in," she said, "and you'd better be good at this, because Carl here's been chewing on his own tail about this for three days, and frankly, so have I."

Michaela Reeves came in, glanced once at the paused footage on the monitor, and something in her expression suggested she'd clearly seen worse and clearly expected to see worse still before this conversation was over.

"Let's talk," she said, and sat down on the edge of the second bed, "about what actually happened in this town on Friday night, and what I think the responsible version of your story looks like once you have the whole picture."

✳

Chapter 80: A Lifetime of Knowing Things You Never Studied

"Some knowledge you earn. Some knowledge simply arrives, uninvited, and asks to be lived with." — attributed to Jonathan Levinson, private notebook

Monday, November 3, 1997: Fondren High School, Gymnasium (temporary shelter), Sunnydale, Evening 8:50pm —

Fondren High's gymnasium had been converted, over the past three days, into the kind of makeshift operation that happened whenever a town quietly needed somewhere to put people and things it wasn't ready to explain to the outside world — cots along one wall, a folding table with a coffee urn that never seemed to run empty, and, in the far corner past the bleachers, three hundred and eighty pounds of extremely content silverback gorilla, currently asleep on a stack of gym mats with his knuckles curled under his chin like a housecat twelve times too large for the metaphor.

Joel Garrity sat cross-legged on the floor a few feet from him, staff across his lap, doing the kind of stillness that wasn't meditation exactly but wasn't not meditation either — the stillness of a man learning to listen to something that had opened up inside his head three days ago and hadn't fully stopped talking since.

"He's snoring again," Amy Madison said, from the folding chair she'd pulled up beside him, not looking up from the paperback she wasn't really reading.

"He does that when he's happy," Joel said. "I looked it up. Well — Dresden looked it up, once, a long time ago, for reasons I don't have access to. Gorillas snore more when they're relaxed. It's a good sign."

"You keep saying that. 'Dresden looked it up.' Like there's a filing cabinet in there and you just walk over and open a drawer."

"It's not that clean," Joel said, honestly. "It's more like — I'll be thinking about something, anything, warding the door, and then I'll just know a thing I never learned. Not remember learning it. Just know it, the way you know your own name." He looked at his hands. "I know forty-three different variants of a basic ward circle. I've never drawn one before Friday. I know what silver nitrate smells like when it's been properly consecrated versus when someone's cutting corners. I have never touched silver nitrate in my life."

Amy was quiet for a moment, and Joel recognized the quiet, because he'd started recognizing it in her over the last three days — the quality of someone about to say something she'd been carrying alone and had finally decided was safe to put down.

"It's not just knowledge, for me," she said. "That's the part nobody's asked about yet, because everyone's very focused on whether I can throw a ward or curse somebody's shoes, which, yes, I can, but that's not the actual — " She stopped. Started again. "I have memories, Joel. Not facts. Memories. A whole life of being a witch that I never lived. Someone's coven. Someone's teacher, an old woman with a bad hip who used to make her climb actual stairs for actual reasons before she'd teach her actual anything. A specific rainy Tuesday in a specific kitchen that smelled like burnt sage, learning to bind a wound closed with nothing but intention and a very kind of patient stubbornness. I remember being afraid of my own power once, a long time ago, in a life I never had, and I remember getting over that fear, slowly, the hard way, over years I didn't live through."

"That's not the same as gaining a skill," Joel said, quiet.

"No," Amy said. "It's not. Jonathan got a soldier's instincts and half his nightmares. I got somebody's entire childhood, entire adulthood, an entire relationship with power that took her a lifetime to build — and I got it all at once, on a Friday, standing in my kitchen in a bad Halloween costume, and none of it ever happened to me." She looked at her own hands, turning them over slowly, the way someone examines hands that suddenly feel like they might not belong to them. "Sometimes I reach for a spell and I don't know if I'm remembering how to do it, or if I'm remembering someone else remembering how to do it. I don't know if there's a difference anymore. I don't know if there's supposed to be."

Joel didn't say anything for a moment. In the corner, Magilla shifted, resettled, exhaled a long low rumbling breath that vibrated faintly through the gym floor.

"I think," Joel said, slowly, "that maybe it doesn't matter where the water came from, once it's actually in the well. You can use it. It's yours to use, regardless of the source, because you're the one standing here deciding what to do with it." He glanced at her. "That's not me being glib. Dresden thought about this a lot, apparently — inherited power, family curses, that whole complicated inheritance thing wizards deal with. The knowledge isn't the danger. What you choose to build with it is."

Amy considered that. "That's annoyingly reasonable."

"I contain several lifetimes of annoyingly reasonable, apparently," Joel said. "It's a whole thing."

A soft snort came from the corner, not from Magilla — the gorilla was still fully and completely asleep — but from further along the bleachers, where a paramedic on her dinner break had been quietly listening to more of this conversation than either of them had noticed.

"Sorry," the paramedic said, not sounding especially sorry. "It's just — you two are having a fascinating philosophical conversation next to a sleeping four-hundred-pound gorilla, and I want you to know I clocked that, and I'm choosing not to ask any follow-up questions, because I've been working this shelter for three days and I've decided that's the healthiest way to get through a shift."

"Wise," Amy said.

"He's very sweet, by the way," the paramedic added, nodding at Magilla. "Ate an entire box of granola bars earlier and then let three different kids braid things into his fur. Never once complained."

"He likes kids," Joel said. "We figured that out day one. He gets agitated if he thinks one of them's upset and can't immediately identify why. It's very specific and very sweet and also occasionally alarming when a four-hundred-pound primate decides he needs to personally investigate why somebody's crying in line for the bathroom."

"Buffy's mom stopped by yesterday," Amy said. "Wanted to see him. He recognized her instantly — got right up, walked over, did the whole gentle-grooming thing he used to do with Buffy. Started checking Joyce's hair for nits like it was the most natural greeting in the world."

"How'd she take that?"

"Cried a little, actually," Amy said, quiet. "Said it was the first time since Friday that something about that night made her laugh instead of just scared her. Then she brought him a fruit basket the next morning, because apparently that's just what Joyce Summers does when something in her life needs feeding."

Joel looked over at the sleeping gorilla, at the sheer domestic absurdity of the whole arrangement — a nine-hundred-year-old vampire aristocracy renovating a manor three miles away, a Terminator meditating in a school library, and, here, in a converted gymnasium, a real silverback gorilla who'd apparently decided his life's calling now involved gentle grooming duties and granola bar appreciation.

"What happens to him," Joel said, "once things settle down. Assuming things settle down."

Amy was quiet for a moment.

"I don't know," she said, honestly. "The Sunnydale Zoo doesn't have anywhere near the setup for a gorilla his size. Sending him to some accredited sanctuary feels — wrong, somehow, given he's not actually feral, he's not actually dangerous, he's basically just an enormous, extremely gentle guy who got picked to carry the weight of somebody's Halloween costume and ended up, I don't know. Real. Whatever real means for something like that." She looked over at him, sleeping peacefully, utterly unbothered by the strangeness of his own continued existence. "I think, for now, he stays with us. With me and Joel. Somebody's got to, and honestly — " she smiled, small and a little surprised at herself, " — I like having him around. He doesn't ask complicated questions about inherited power and borrowed memory. He just wants granola bars and somebody's hair to check for nits."

"Lower bar," Joel agreed. "Healthier, in some ways."

Magilla, as if sensing himself being discussed, shifted, cracked one eye open, surveyed the two of them with the mild, unbothered curiosity of a creature entirely at peace with a life he never could have predicted, and then closed his eye again and went back to snoring.

"Yeah," Amy said, watching him settle. "That's the whole philosophy, right there."

✳

Chapter 81: Sixteen Years Doesn't Prepare You for a Gorilla, But It Helps

"Sixteen years will teach you plenty. A gorilla will teach you the rest in about four minutes." — attributed to Buffy Summers

Monday, November 3, 1997: Fondren High School, Gymnasium (temporary shelter),
Sunnydale, Evening 9:05pm —

Elena Diaz had left Los Angeles County EMS in 1994 for the deliberate reason that she wanted to spend the back half of her career somewhere quiet, and she had chosen Sunnydale, of all the small California towns that might have offered that, because the housing was affordable, the commute to her sister's place in Oxnard was reasonable, and the crime statistics — official ones, the kind that made it into the packet the county gave her — had looked, on paper, remarkably unremarkable.

She had been in this town three years now. She had stopped, sometime around year two, being surprised by the unremarkable statistics not quite matching what she actually saw on shift, and had developed, the way you develop calluses, a personal policy of simply not looking too hard at the gap between the two.

That policy had lasted until last Friday night, when the gap had stopped being a gap and become, instead, an entire town's worth of impossible things happening in the open, all at once, and Elena Diaz — sixteen years an EMT and paramedic, four hundred plus documented calls a year for over a decade and a half, a woman who had believed there was no category of emergency scene left that could still surprise her — had spent the single strangest overnight shift of her professional life triaging patients for injuries that made no earthly sense, in a town that had apparently decided, collectively, not to discuss any of it afterward.

She had not discussed it either. Not with her sister. Not with her supervisor, who had handed her the Fondren High shelter assignment three days ago with a specific careful blankness in his face that told her, more clearly than any briefing could have, that this was a need-to-know situation and she had, apparently, been quietly moved onto the need-to-know list without anyone formally telling her so.

She had decided, around hour six of that first impossible night, that the only way through this particular professional chapter was the same way she'd gotten through every other hard chapter in sixteen years — one patient at a time, one set of vitals at a time, and a personal, ironclad refusal to let the size of the mystery distract her from the size of the job actually in front of her.

Tonight the job in front of her included: seven minor lacerations, two sprained ankles, one dislocated shoulder that had gone back in clean, a low-grade fever in a nine-year-old that was almost certainly just an ordinary nine-year-old's virus and not, thank God, anything stranger, and one very large, very gentle, real gorilla who required, apparently, the same basic monitoring she'd give any other unusually calm large mammal patient — weight, temperature, appetite, general demeanor.

She sat now on the low bleacher a few feet from where Joel Garrity and the girl he called Amy had been having what was, without exaggeration, one of the more thoughtful conversations about the nature of borrowed memory she'd overheard in her entire career, and found herself, for a moment, simply watching them — two teenagers, seventeen at most, discussing inherited trauma and the philosophy of consent regarding knowledge you never chose to carry, with the calm fluency of people twice their age, while a gorilla snored contentedly six feet away.

She'd stopped being surprised by scenes like this three days ago. She had not, however, stopped finding them remarkable, which felt, to her, like an important distinction to maintain — the difference between numbness and simple professional composure. Numbness scared her. She'd watched colleagues burn out into it over the years, watched them stop being able to feel the weight of what they were actually witnessing, and she had promised herself, a long time ago, back in her rookie year in East LA, that she would rather feel the full weight of every strange and terrible thing she saw than protect herself by going numb to it.

So she felt it. All of it. The strangeness. A quiet grief underneath it — kids who'd had entire other lives grafted into their heads overnight, kids managing weight no seventeen-year-old should have to carry, a town's worth of families quietly, carefully, not discussing what had actually happened to their children. She felt all of it, every shift, and she coped the only way she'd ever known how to cope with anything too large to look at directly: she did the next concrete task in front of her, and then the one after that, and she trusted that the accumulation of small, specific, competent actions would eventually add up to something that resembled getting through it.

"Sorry," Diaz said, not sounding especially sorry, catching Joel and Amy both glancing her way. "It's just — you two are having a genuinely fascinating philosophical conversation next to a sleeping four-hundred-pound gorilla, and I want you to know I clocked that, and I'm choosing not to ask any follow-up questions, because I've been working this shelter for three days and I've decided that's the healthiest way to get through a shift."

"Smart," Amy said.

"Sixteen years in emergency medicine," Diaz said, checking something on her clipboard without urgency. "You learn the questions that help the patient and the questions that just help your own nerves, and you learn to only ask the first kind. Guy shows up with a gorilla he's apparently fostering with his witch friend, that's a second-kind question, every time." She nodded at Magilla, still snoring contentedly against the gym mats. "He's due for another checkup tomorrow, by the way. Dr. Osei wants weight and temperature again, said his numbers looked good Thursday but she likes a pattern before she stops worrying."

"You've been checking on him personally," Joel said.

"Somebody's got to, and frankly he's the easiest patient in this entire building," Diaz said, with the dry affection of someone who'd clearly become fonder of the arrangement than she was strictly willing to admit. "He doesn't argue about his vitals. He doesn't try to leave against medical advice. World's best patient, honestly, present company held to a much lower standard." She looked pointedly at Joel's staff, at the faint singed patch on his sleeve he hadn't yet explained to anyone. "Speaking of which — that burn on your forearm. When were you going to mention that."

"It's minor."

"That's also a second-kind answer," Diaz said, already reaching for her bag. "Sit still. Gorilla whisperer or not, you're still on my list tonight."

Her radio crackled. A minor call — a scraped knee, two bunks over, a kid who'd fallen off a cot trying to reach a granola bar on a high shelf, the mundane injury that felt, some nights, like the kindest thing in the entire building.

She finished with Joel's burn first, quick and efficient, then stood, gathered her kit, glanced once more at the sleeping gorilla, and headed toward the scraped knee with the same steady, unhurried competence she brought to everything, because that competence was, she'd decided three years ago and confirmed three days ago, the only thing in this entire strange profession that had never once let her down.

"Diaz," she said, quietly, to herself, the way she sometimes did on hard shifts, a small private ritual of naming herself back into her own body when the job got large enough to blur the edges. "You're still here. Still doing the job. That's enough for tonight."

It was enough. It had to be. She had four more hours on shift, a gorilla due for vitals, and, somewhere across town, she suspected, though nobody had told her directly, a great deal more of this strange, impossible, quietly beautiful mess still unfolding, one concrete task at a time.

She got to work.

✳

Chapter 82: What the Ghost Roads Sound Like When You're Not Using Them

"The dead don't actually rest easy. They just stop being in a hurry." — Murphy's Laws of Combat

Monday, November 3, 1997: Rosenberg Residence, Sunnydale, Evening 9:00pm —

Willow Rosenberg's bedroom had, over the past three days, developed a very specific problem, which was that she could no longer sit in it after dark without being fully aware of exactly how many walls stood between her and the outside, because for one entire night on October 31st she had known, personally and completely, what it was like to have no walls stand between her and anything at all.

She'd tried explaining this to her parents. She had not succeeded. Sheila and Ira Rosenberg had returned from their conference in Pasadena two days after Halloween to a daughter who insisted, calmly and specifically, that she was fine, and who had also rearranged her entire bedroom so that her bed no longer faced the closet, for reasons she had not been able to articulate in any way that didn't sound insane.

She was not fine. She was working on it. There was a difference, and she'd started, over the last three days, to actually believe there was a path between the two.

"You've been looking at that closet for a while now," Jenny Calendar said, from the desk chair, where she'd been sitting for the better part of an hour going through a stack of books that Giles had loaned her with the caveat that several of them were extremely old and she should handle the bindings gently.

"Sorry. Zoned out."

"You went very quiet and looked at it like it had said something to you," Jenny said, not unkindly. "I've been a teacher for three years, Willow. I notice when a student's somewhere else."

Willow looked at the closet. It hadn't said anything. It never said anything.
